52.         // Returns the first SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback supported by the provided SimpleHapticsController
53.         // which supports the specified waveform.
54.         public static SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback FindSupportedFeedback(SimpleHapticsController hapticsController, ushort waveform)
55.         {
56.             foreach (SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback feedback in hapticsController.SupportedFeedback)
57.             {
58.                 if (feedback.Waveform == waveform)
59.                 {
60.                     return feedback;
61.                 }
62.             }
63.             return null;
64.         }

15. // This sample shows how the default InkCanvas supports tactile signals.
16. // Simply begin inking in the InkCanvas with a supported pen and tactile signals
17. // will automatically be played for each of the different brush types.
18. namespace SDKTemplate
19. {
20.     public sealed partial class Scenario1_InkCanvasTactileFeedback : Page
21.     {
22.

24.         {
25.             InitializeComponent();
26.         }

1. //*********************************************************
2. //
3. // Copyright (c) Microsoft. All rights reserved.
4. // This code is licensed under the MIT License (MIT).
5. // THIS CODE IS PROVIDED *AS IS* WITHOUT WARRANTY OF

8. // PURPOSE, MERCHANTABILITY, OR NON-INFRINGEMENT.

11. 
12. 
13. using System.Numerics;
14. using Windows.Devices.Haptics;
15. using Windows.Devices.Input;
16. using Windows.Foundation;
17. using Windows.UI;
18. using Windows.UI.Xaml;
19. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Controls;
20. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Input;
21. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Media;
22. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Shapes;
23. 
24. // This sample shows how to use Inking and Interaction tactile signals in
25. // conjunction with each other.
26. // Namely, this sample plays the InkContinuous waveform when dragging a movable
27. // rectangle and then plays the Click waveform when the rectangle is in range
28. // of a snap gridline and, finally, if the movable rectangle snaps to a gridline
29. // upon release, it plays the Click waveform one more time.
30. namespace SDKTemplate
31. {
32.     public sealed partial class Scenario5_InkingAndInteractionFeedback : Page
33.     {
34.         private readonly int gridSize = 100;
35.         readonly double snapDistance = 20;
36.         private Point topLeft;
37.         private Point previewTopLeft = new Point(-1, -1);
38.         private TransformGroup transforms;
39.         private MatrixTransform previousTransform;
40.         private CompositeTransform deltaTransform;
41.         PenDevice penDevice;
42.         SimpleHapticsController hapticsController;
43. 
44.         // Set the initial configuration of the canvas and the rectangles
45.         public Scenario5_InkingAndInteractionFeedback()
46.         {
47.             InitializeComponent();
48.             InitializeGridLines();
49.             InitializeManipulationTransforms();
50.         }
51. 
52.         // Draw gridlines based on the gridSize
53.         private void InitializeGridLines()
54.         {
55.             SolidColorBrush darkGray = new SolidColorBrush(Colors.DarkGray);
56.             double width = hapticCanvas.Width;
57.             double height = hapticCanvas.Height;
58.             for (int i = 0; i <= width; i += gridSize)
59.             {
60.                 Line currLine = new Line() {
61.                     Stroke = darkGray,
62.                     X1 = i,
63.                     X2 = i,
64.                     Y1 = 0,
65.                     Y2 = height,
66.                 };
67.                 hapticCanvas.Children.Add(currLine);
68.             }
69.             for (int i = 0; i <= height; i += gridSize)
70.             {
71.                 Line currLine = new Line() {
72.                     Stroke = darkGray,
73.                     X1 = 0,
74.                     X2 = width,
75.                     Y1 = i,
76.                     Y2 = i,
77.                 };
78.                 hapticCanvas.Children.Add(currLine);
79.             }
80.         }
81. 
82.         // Configure the Manipulation transforms that are used to drag the rectangle around
83.         private void InitializeManipulationTransforms()
84.         {
85.             previousTransform = new MatrixTransform() { Matrix = Matrix.Identity };
86.             topLeft = new Point(75, 75);
87.             deltaTransform = new CompositeTransform() { TranslateX = topLeft.X, TranslateY = topLeft.Y };
88. 
89.             transforms = new TransformGroup() { Children = { previousTransform, deltaTransform } };
90. 
91.             // Set the render transform on the rect
92.             movableRect.RenderTransform = transforms;
93.         }
94. 
95.         // The PointerEnter event will get fired as soon as Pointer input is received in the movableRect.
96.         // This event handler implementation will query the device providing input to see if it's a pen and
97.         // then check to see the pen supports tactile feedback and, if so, configure the PenDevice
98.         // to send the InkContinuous tactile signal.
99.         private void MovableRect_Entered(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
100.         {
101.             // If the current Pointer device is not a pen, exit
102.             if (e.Pointer.PointerDeviceType != PointerDeviceType.Pen)
103.             {
104.                 return;
105.             }
106. 
107.             // Attempt to retrieve the PenDevice from the current PointerId
108.             penDevice = PenDevice.GetFromPointerId(e.Pointer.PointerId);
109. 
110.             // If a PenDevice cannot be retrieved based on the PointerId, it does not support
111.             // advanced pen features, such as tactile feedback
112.             if (penDevice == null)
113.             {
114.                 statusText.Text = "Advanced pen features not supported";
115.                 return;
116.             }
117. 
118.             // Check to see if the current PenDevice supports tactile feedback by seeing if it
119.             // has a SimpleHapticsController
120.             hapticsController = penDevice.SimpleHapticsController;
121.             if (hapticsController == null)
122.             {
123.                 statusText.Text = "This pen does not provide tactile feedback";
124.                 return;
125.             }
126. 
127.             // Send the InkContinuous waveform to the PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController.
128.             // Once sent, inking continuous tactile feedback will be triggered as soon as the pen tip touches
129.             // the screen and will be stopped once the pen tip is lifted from the screen.
130.             // Also, check to see if the current PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController supports
131.             // setting the intensity value of the tactile feedback.  If so, set it to 0.75.
132.             // If not, send the waveform with the default intensity.
133.             foreach (var waveform in hapticsController.SupportedFeedback)
134.             {
135.                 if (waveform.Waveform == KnownSimpleHapticsControllerWaveforms.InkContinuous)
136.                 {
137.                     if (hapticsController.IsIntensitySupported)
138.                     {
139.                         hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(waveform, 0.75);
140.                      }
141.                     else
142.                     {
143.                         hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(waveform);
144.                     }
145.                 }
146.             }
147. 
148.         }
149. 
150.         // Stop sending the tactile feedback and clear the current penDevice and hapticsController on PointerExit.
151.         // Stopping the feedback is important as it clears the tactile signal from the PenDevice's
152.         // SimpleHapticsController, ensuring that it has a clean state once it next enters range.
153.         private void MovableRect_Exited(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
154.         {
155.             penDevice = null;
156.             statusText.Text = "";
157.             if (hapticsController != null)
158.             {
159.                 hapticsController.StopFeedback();
160.                 hapticsController = null;
161.             }
162.         }
163. 
164.         // Change the color of the movableRect once a manipulation is started. This coincides with the pen
165.         // touching down inside the rectangle, so this color change will happen at the same time that the
166.         // InkContinuous waveform begins playing.
167.         private void MovableRect_ManipulationStarted(object sender, ManipulationStartedRoutedEventArgs e)
168.         {
169.             movableRect.Style = MovableRectangleDragged;
170.         }
171. 
172.         // Update the position of the movableRect and update the previewRect as needed
173.         private void MovableRect_ManipulationDelta(object sender, ManipulationDeltaRoutedEventArgs e)
174.         {
175.             previousTransform.Matrix = transforms.Value;
176. 
177.             // Look at the Delta property of the ManipulationDeltaRoutedEventArgs to retrieve the X and Y changes
178.             deltaTransform.TranslateX = e.Delta.Translation.X;
179.             deltaTransform.TranslateY = e.Delta.Translation.Y;
180.             topLeft.X += e.Delta.Translation.X;
181.             topLeft.Y += e.Delta.Translation.Y;
182.             UpdatePreviewRect();
183.         }
184. 
185.         // Update the visibilty or position of the previewRect based on the location
186.         // of the movableRect.
187.         // If the previewRect is snapped to a given gridline for the first time,
188.         // play the Click tactile signal.
189.         private void UpdatePreviewRect()
190.         {
191.             if (IsInSnapRange())
192.             {
193.                 previewRect.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;
194.                 Point newPreviewTopLeft = GetSnappedPoint();
195. 
196.                 // Check to see if this is the first time that the previewRect is in range of a given gridline
197.                 // and send the Click tactile signal if so
198.                 bool sendHaptics = true;
199.                 if ((previewTopLeft.X == newPreviewTopLeft.X && previewTopLeft.Y == newPreviewTopLeft.Y) ||
200.                     (previewTopLeft.X == newPreviewTopLeft.X && newPreviewTopLeft.Y == topLeft.Y) ||
201.                     (previewTopLeft.Y == newPreviewTopLeft.Y && newPreviewTopLeft.X == topLeft.X))
202.                 {
203.                     sendHaptics = false;
204.                 }
205.                 if (previewTopLeft.X != newPreviewTopLeft.X || previewTopLeft.Y != newPreviewTopLeft.Y)
206.                 {
207.                     previewTopLeft = newPreviewTopLeft;
208.                     previewRect.Translation = new Vector3((float)previewTopLeft.X, (float)previewTopLeft.Y, 0);
209.                     if (hapticsController != null && sendHaptics)
210.                     {
211.                         foreach (var waveform in hapticsController.SupportedFeedback)
212.                         {
213.                             if (waveform.Waveform == KnownSimpleHapticsControllerWaveforms.Click)
214.                             {
215.                                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(waveform);
216.                             }
217.                         }
218.                     }
219.                 }
220.             }
221.             else
222.             {
223.                 previewRect.Visibility = Visibility.Collapsed;
224.                 previewTopLeft = new Point(-1, -1);
225.             }
226.         }
227. 
228.         // When the manipulation is over, reset the color of the movableRect and
229.         // snap it to a gridline if appropriate
230.         private void MovableRect_ManipulationCompleted(object sender, ManipulationCompletedRoutedEventArgs e)
231.         {
232.             movableRect.Style = MovableRectangle;
233.             if (IsInSnapRange())
234.             {
235.                 SnapMovableRectToGridLines();
236.             }
237.         }
238.         
239.         // Updates the position of the movableRect to snap to the appropriate gridline(s).
240.         // In addition, play the Click tactile signal to provide additional feedback to the user that it has snapped.
241.         private void SnapMovableRectToGridLines()
242.         {
243.             topLeft = GetSnappedPoint();
244.             previousTransform = new MatrixTransform() { Matrix = Matrix.Identity };
245.             deltaTransform.TranslateX = topLeft.X;
246.             deltaTransform.TranslateY = topLeft.Y;
247. 
248.             transforms = new TransformGroup() { Children = { previousTransform, deltaTransform } };
249. 
250.             movableRect.RenderTransform = transforms;
251. 
252.             if (hapticsController != null)
253.             {
254.                 foreach (var waveform in hapticsController.SupportedFeedback)
255.                 {
256.                     if (waveform.Waveform == KnownSimpleHapticsControllerWaveforms.Click)
257.                     {
258.                         hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(waveform);
259.                     }
260.                 }
261.             }
262.         }
263. 
264.         // Retrieve the point corresponding to the top-left corner of where the movableRect would snap to
265.         // if released.
266.         private Point GetSnappedPoint()
267.         {
268.             Point snappedPoint;
269.             if (topLeft.X % gridSize <= snapDistance)
270.             {
271.                 snappedPoint.X = topLeft.X + topLeft.X % gridSize * -1;
272.             }
273.             else if (topLeft.X % gridSize >= gridSize - snapDistance)
274.             {
275.                 snappedPoint.X = topLeft.X + gridSize - (topLeft.X % gridSize);
276.             }
277.             else
278.             {
279.                 snappedPoint.X = topLeft.X;
280.             }
281.             if (topLeft.Y % gridSize <= snapDistance)
282.             {
283.                 snappedPoint.Y = topLeft.Y + topLeft.Y % gridSize * -1;
284.             }
285.             else if (topLeft.Y % gridSize >= gridSize - snapDistance)
286.             {
287.                 snappedPoint.Y = topLeft.Y + gridSize - (topLeft.Y % gridSize);
288.             }
289.             else
290.             {
291.                 snappedPoint.Y = topLeft.Y;
292.             }
293.             return snappedPoint;
294.         }
295. 
296.         // Check to see if the movableRect is currently within snapping range of a gridline
297.         private bool IsInSnapRange()
298.         {
299.             return topLeft.X % gridSize <= snapDistance ||
300.                 topLeft.X % gridSize >= gridSize - snapDistance ||
301.                 topLeft.Y % gridSize <= snapDistance ||
302.                 topLeft.Y % gridSize >= gridSize - snapDistance;
303.         }
304.     }
305. }
306.

12. using System.Collections.Generic;
13. using Windows.Devices.Haptics;
14. using Windows.Devices.Input;
15. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Controls;
16. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Input;
17. 
18. // This sample shows how to query the pen currently providing input to the system to see
19. // if it supports tactile feedback and, if so, what features it supports
20. namespace SDKTemplate
21. {
22.     public sealed partial class Scenario2_SupportedTactileFeedback : Page
23.     {
24.         SimpleHapticsController hapticsController;
25.         PenDevice penDevice;
26. 
27.         public Scenario2_SupportedTactileFeedback()
28.         {
29.             InitializeComponent();
30.         }
31. 
32.         // The PointerEnter event will get fired as soon as Pointer input is received.
33.         // This event handler implementation will query the device providing input to see if it's a pen and
34.         // then check to see the pen supports tactile feedback and, if so, what features it supports
35.         private void Pointer_Entered(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
36.         {
37.             // If the current Pointer device is not a pen, exit
38.             if (e.Pointer.PointerDeviceType != PointerDeviceType.Pen)
39.             {
40.                 supportedFeatures.Text = "";
41.                 supportedFeedback.Text = "";
42.                 return;
43.             }
44. 
45.             // Attempt to retrieve the PenDevice from the current PointerId
46.             penDevice = PenDevice.GetFromPointerId(e.Pointer.PointerId);
47. 
48.             // If a PenDevice cannot be retrieved based on the PointerId, it does not support
49.             // advanced pen features, such as tactile feedback
50.             if (penDevice == null)
51.             {
52.                 statusText.Text = "Advanced pen features not supported";
53.                 supportedFeatures.Text = "";
54.                 supportedFeedback.Text = "";
55.                 return;
56.             }
57. 
58.             // Check to see if the current PenDevice supports tactile feedback by seeing if it
59.             // has a SimpleHapticsController
60.             hapticsController = penDevice.SimpleHapticsController;
61.             if (hapticsController == null)
62.             {
63.                 statusText.Text = "This pen does not provide tactile feedback";
64.                 return;
65.             }
66. 
67.             // Check which tactile feedback features are supported
68.             supportedFeatures.Text = "Supported Haptics Features:\n";
69.             if (hapticsController.IsIntensitySupported)
70.             {
71.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "Intensity\n";
72.             }
73.             if (hapticsController.IsPlayCountSupported)
74.             {
75.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "PlayCount\n";
76.             }
77.             if (hapticsController.IsPlayDurationSupported)
78.             {
79.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "PlayDuration\n";
80.             }
81.             if (hapticsController.IsReplayPauseIntervalSupported)
82.             {
83.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "ReplayPauseInterval\n";
84.             }
85. 
86.             // Check which feedback waveforms are supported
87.             supportedFeedback.Text = "Supported Feedback:\n";
88.             foreach (SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback feedback in hapticsController.SupportedFeedback)
89.             {
90.                 ushort waveform = feedback.Waveform;
91.                 foreach (KeyValuePair<string, ushort> entry in MainPage.WaveformNamesMap)
92.                 {
93.                     if (entry.Value == waveform)
94.                     {
95.                         supportedFeedback.Text += entry.Key + "\n";
96.                         break;
97.                     }
98.                 }
99.             }
100.             statusText.Text = "";
101.         }
102. 
103.         // Clear the current penDevice and hapticsController on PointerExit
104.         private void Pointer_Exited(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
105.         {
106.             penDevice = null;
107.             hapticsController = null;
108.         }
109.     }
110. }
111.

12. using Windows.Devices.Haptics;
13. using Windows.Devices.Input;
14. using Windows.UI.Xaml;
15. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Controls;
16. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Input;
17. 
18. // This sample shows how to play Interaction feedback tactile signals, how to
19. // adjust the intensity of the tactile signal, and how to fall back to a default
20. // waveform if the desired waveform is not supported by the current PenDevice
21. namespace SDKTemplate
22. {
23.     public sealed partial class Scenario4_InteractionFeedback : Page
24.     {
25.         SimpleHapticsController hapticsController;
26.         PenDevice penDevice;
27.         SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback currentFeedback;
28. 
29.         public Scenario4_InteractionFeedback()
30.         {
31.             InitializeComponent();
32.         }
33. 
34.         // The PointerEnter event will get fired as soon as Pointer input is received.
35.         // This event handler implementation will query the device providing input to see if it's a pen and
36.         // then check to see the pen supports tactile feedback
37.         private void MainGrid_Entered(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
38.         {
39.             // If the current Pointer device is not a pen, exit
40.             if (e.Pointer.PointerDeviceType != PointerDeviceType.Pen)
41.             {
42.                 return;
43.             }
44. 
45.             // Attempt to retrieve the PenDevice from the current PointerId
46.             penDevice = PenDevice.GetFromPointerId(e.Pointer.PointerId);
47. 
48.             // If a PenDevice cannot be retrieved based on the PointerId, it does not support
49.             // advanced pen features, such as tactile feedback
50.             if (penDevice == null)
51.             {
52.                 statusText.Text = "Advanced pen features not supported";
53.                 return;
54.             }
55. 
56.             // Check to see if the current PenDevice supports tactile feedback by seeing if it
57.             // has a SimpleHapticsController
58.             hapticsController = penDevice.SimpleHapticsController;
59.             if (hapticsController == null)
60.             {
61.                 statusText.Text = "This pen does not provide tactile feedback";
62.                 return;
63.             }
64.         }
65. 
66.         // Stop sending the tactile feedback and clear the current penDevice and hapticsController on PointerExit.
67.         // Stopping the feedback is important as it clears the tactile signal from the PenDevice's
68.         // SimpleHapticsController, ensuring that it has a clean state once it next enters range.
69.         private void MainGrid_Exited(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
70.         {
71.             penDevice = null;
72.             statusText.Text = "";
73.             if (hapticsController != null)
74.             {
75.                 hapticsController.StopFeedback();
76.                 hapticsController = null;
77.                 currentFeedback = null;
78.             }
79.         }
80. 
81.         // Send haptic feedback that matches the selected waveform upon the button being clicked
82.         private void SendFeedback_Clicked(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)
83.         {
84.             if (hapticsController == null)
85.             {
86.                 return;
87.             }
88. 
89.             // Get feedback based on the user's selected waveform
90.             currentFeedback = GetSelectedFeedbackOrFallback(out string message);
91. 
92.             // Send the current feedback to the PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController.
93.             // Once sent, unlike inking feedback, this feedback will be played immediately.
94.             // Also, check to see if the current PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController supports
95.             // setting the intensity value of the tactile feedback.  If so, set it based
96.             // on the slider.  If not, send the feedback without custom intensity.
97.             if (hapticsController.IsIntensitySupported)
98.             {
99.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback, intensitySlider.Value / 100);
100.                 message += "\nIntensity set to " + intensitySlider.Value + "%";
101.             }
102.             else
103.             {
104.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback);
105.                 message += "\nSetting intensity is not supported by this pen";
106.             }
107.             statusText.Text = message;
108.         }
109. 
110.         // Get feedback which matches the currently selected waveform in the dropdown.
111.         // To get the feedback, the SimpleHapticsController's SupportedFeedback list must be traversed
112.         // to see if a matching feedback is present.
113.         // This is important because Click is the only Interaction waveform required to be
114.         // supported by every pen with haptics.
115.         // If no matching feedback is found in the SupportedFeedback list, this method returns
116.         // Click as a guaranteed fallback.
117.         private SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback GetSelectedFeedbackOrFallback(out string message)
118.         {
119.             // Look up the waveform the user selected.
120.             string name = (string)waveformComboBox.SelectionBoxItem;
121.             ushort waveform = MainPage.WaveformNamesMap[name];
122. 
123.             // See if the haptics controller supports the selected waveform.
124.             SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback feedback = MainPage.FindSupportedFeedback(hapticsController, waveform);
125.             if (feedback != null)
126.             {
127.                 message = "Waveform set to " + name;
128.                 return feedback;
129.             }
130. 
131.             // It does not. Use Click as a fallback.
132.             message = name + " is not supported by this pen, so falling back to Click";
133.             return MainPage.FindSupportedFeedback(hapticsController, KnownSimpleHapticsControllerWaveforms.Click);
134.         }
135.     }
136. }

25.         PenDevice penDevice;

29.         {
30.             InitializeComponent();
31.         }
32. 
33.         // The PointerEnter event will get fired as soon as Pointer input is received.
34.         // This event handler implementation will query the device providing input to see if it's a pen and
35.         // then check to see the pen supports tactile feedback and, if so, configure the PenDevice
36.         // to send the appropriate tactile signal based on what is selected in the dropdown.
37.         private void HapticCanvas_Entered(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
38.         {
39.             // If the current Pointer device is not a pen, exit
40.             if (e.Pointer.PointerDeviceType != PointerDeviceType.Pen)
41.             {
42.                 return;
43.             }
44. 
45.             // Attempt to retrieve the PenDevice from the current PointerId
46.             penDevice = PenDevice.GetFromPointerId(e.Pointer.PointerId);
47. 
48.             // If a PenDevice cannot be retrieved based on the PointerId, it does not support
49.             // advanced pen features, such as tactile feedback
50.             if (penDevice == null)
51.             {
52.                 statusText.Text = "Advanced pen features not supported";
53.                 return;
54.             }
55. 
56.             // Check to see if the current PenDevice supports tactile feedback by seeing if it
57.             // has a SimpleHapticsController
58.             hapticsController = penDevice.SimpleHapticsController;
59.             if (hapticsController == null)
60.             {
61.                 statusText.Text = "This pen does not provide tactile feedback";
62.                 return;
63.             }
64. 
65.             // Get feedback based on the user's selected waveform
66.             currentFeedback = GetSelectedFeedbackOrFallback(out string message);
67. 
68.             // Send the current feedback to the PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController.
69.             // Once sent, inking tactile feedback will be triggered as soon as the pen tip touches
70.             // the screen and will be stopped once the pen tip is lifted from the screen.
71.             // Also, check to see if the current PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController supports
72.             // setting the intensity value of the tactile feedback.  If so, set it based
73.             // on the slider.  If not, send the waveform without custom intensity.
74.             if (hapticsController.IsIntensitySupported)
75.             {
76.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback, intensitySlider.Value / 100);
77.                 message += "\nIntensity set to " + intensitySlider.Value + "%";
78.             }
79.             else
80.             {
81.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback);
82.                 message += "\nSetting intensity is not supported by this pen";
83.             }
84.             statusText.Text = message;
85.         }
86. 
87.         // Stop sending the tactile feedback and clear the current penDevice and hapticsController on PointerExit.
88.         // Stopping the feedback is important as it clears the tactile signal from the PenDevice's
89.         // SimpleHapticsController, ensuring that it has a clean state once it next enters range.
90.         private void HapticCanvas_Exited(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
91.         {

93.             statusText.Text = "";
94.             if (hapticsController != null)
95.             {
96.                 hapticsController.StopFeedback();
97.                 hapticsController = null;
98.                 currentFeedback = null;
99.             }
100.         }
101. 
102.         // Get feedback which matches the currently selected waveform in the dropdown.
103.         // To get the feedback, the SimpleHapticsController's SupportedFeedback list must be traversed
104.         // to see if a matching feedback is present.
105.         // This is important because InkContinuous is the only Inking waveform required to be
106.         // supported by every pen with haptics.
107.         // If no matching feedback is found in the SupportedFeedback list, this method returns
108.         // InkContinuous as a guaranteed fallback.

12. using System;
13. using Windows.ApplicationModel.Activation;
14. using Windows.UI.Xaml;

18. // The Blank Application template is documented at http://go.microsoft.com/fwlink/?LinkId=402347&clcid=0x409
19. 
20. namespace SDKTemplate
21. {
22.     /// <summary>
23.     /// Provides application-specific behavior to supplement the default Application class.
24.     /// </summary>
25.     sealed partial class App : Application
26.     {
27.         /// <summary>
28.         /// Initializes the singleton application object.  This is the first line of authored code
29.         /// executed, and as such is the logical equivalent of main() or WinMain().
30.         /// </summary>
31.         public App()
32.         {
33.             this.InitializeComponent();
34.             this.Construct();
35.         }
36. 
37.         /// <summary>
38.         /// Invoked when the application is launched normally by the end user.  Other entry points
39.         /// will be used such as when the application is launched to open a specific file.
40.         /// </summary>
41.         /// <param name="e">Details about the launch request and process.</param>
42.         protected override void OnLaunched(LaunchActivatedEventArgs e)
43.         {
44. 
45. #if DEBUG
46.             if (System.Diagnostics.Debugger.IsAttached)
47.             {
48.                 this.DebugSettings.EnableFrameRateCounter = false;
49.             }
50. #endif
51. 
52.             Frame rootFrame = Window.Current.Content as Frame;
53. 
54.             // Do not repeat app initialization when the Window already has content,
55.             // just ensure that the window is active
56.             if (rootFrame == null)
57.             {
58.                 // Create a Frame to act as the navigation context and navigate to the first page
59.                 rootFrame = new Frame();
60.                 // Set the default language
61.                 rootFrame.Language = Windows.Globalization.ApplicationLanguages.Languages[0];
62. 
63.                 rootFrame.NavigationFailed += OnNavigationFailed;
64. 
65.                 if (e.PreviousExecutionState == ApplicationExecutionState.Terminated)
66.                 {
67.                     //TODO: Load state from previously suspended application
68.                 }
69. 
70.                 // Place the frame in the current Window
71.                 Window.Current.Content = rootFrame;
72.             }
73. 
74.             if (rootFrame.Content == null)
75.             {
76.                 // When the navigation stack isn't restored navigate to the first page,
77.                 // configuring the new page by passing required information as a navigation
78.                 // parameter
79.                 rootFrame.Navigate(typeof(MainPage), e.Arguments);
80.             }
81. 
82.             // Ensure the current window is active
83.             Window.Current.Activate();
84. 
85.             // Some samples want access to the LaunchActivatedEventArgs.
86.             LaunchCompleted(e);
87.         }
88. 
89.         private Frame CreateRootFrame()
90.         {
91.             Frame rootFrame = Window.Current.Content as Frame;
92. 
93.             // Do not repeat app initialization when the Window already has content,
94.             // just ensure that the window is active
95.             if (rootFrame == null)
96.             {
97.                 // Create a Frame to act as the navigation context and navigate to the first page
98.                 rootFrame = new Frame();
99. 
100.                 // Set the default language
101.                 rootFrame.Language = Windows.Globalization.ApplicationLanguages.Languages[0];
102.                 rootFrame.NavigationFailed += OnNavigationFailed;
103. 
104.                 // Place the frame in the current Window
105.                 Window.Current.Content = rootFrame;
106.             }
107. 
108.             return rootFrame;
109.         }
110. 
111.         /// <summary>
112.         /// Invoked when Navigation to a certain page fails
113.         /// </summary>
114.         /// <param name="sender">The Frame which failed navigation</param>
115.         /// <param name="e">Details about the navigation failure</param>
116.         void OnNavigationFailed(object sender, NavigationFailedEventArgs e)
117.         {
118.             throw new Exception("Failed to load Page " + e.SourcePageType.FullName);
119.         }
120. 
121.         // Add any application constructor code in here.
122.         partial void Construct();
123. 
124.         // Add any OnLaunched customization here.
125.         partial void LaunchCompleted(LaunchActivatedEventArgs e);
126.     }

12. using System;
13. using System.Collections.Generic;
14. using Windows.UI.Core;
15. using Windows.UI.Xaml;
16. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Automation.Peers;
17. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Controls;
18. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Data;
19. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Media;
20. using Windows.UI.Xaml.Navigation;
21. 
22. // The Blank Page item template is documented at http://go.microsoft.com/fwlink/?LinkId=402352&clcid=0x409
23. 
24. namespace SDKTemplate
25. {
26.     /// <summary>
27.     /// An empty page that can be used on its own or navigated to within a Frame.
28.     /// </summary>
29.     public sealed partial class MainPage : Page
30.     {
31.         public static MainPage Current;
32. 
33.         public MainPage()
34.         {
35.             this.InitializeComponent();
36. 
37.             // This is a static public property that allows downstream pages to get a handle to the MainPage instance
38.             // in order to call methods that are in this class.
39.             Current = this;
40.             SampleTitle.Text = FEATURE_NAME;
41.         }
42. 
43.         protected override void OnNavigatedTo(NavigationEventArgs e)
44.         {
45.             // Populate the scenario list from the SampleConfiguration.cs file
46.             var itemCollection = new List<Scenario>();
47.             int i = 1;
48.             foreach (Scenario s in scenarios)
49.             {
50.                 itemCollection.Add(new Scenario { Title = $"{i++}) {s.Title}", ClassType = s.ClassType });
51.             }
52.             ScenarioControl.ItemsSource = itemCollection;
53. 
54.             if (Window.Current.Bounds.Width < 640)
55.             {
56.                 ScenarioControl.SelectedIndex = -1;
57.             }
58.             else
59.             {
60.                 ScenarioControl.SelectedIndex = 0;
61.             }
62.         }
63. 
64.         /// <summary>
65.         /// Called whenever the user changes selection in the scenarios list.  This method will navigate to the respective
66.         /// sample scenario page.
67.         /// </summary>
68.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
69.         /// <param name="e"></param>
70.         private void ScenarioControl_SelectionChanged(object sender, SelectionChangedEventArgs e)
71.         {
72.             // Clear the status block when navigating scenarios.
73.             NotifyUser(String.Empty, NotifyType.StatusMessage);
74. 
75.             ListBox scenarioListBox = sender as ListBox;
76.             Scenario s = scenarioListBox.SelectedItem as Scenario;
77.             if (s != null)
78.             {
79.                 ScenarioFrame.Navigate(s.ClassType);
80.                 if (Window.Current.Bounds.Width < 640)
81.                 {
82.                     Splitter.IsPaneOpen = false;
83.                 }
84.             }
85.         }
86. 
87.         public List<Scenario> Scenarios
88.         {
89.             get { return this.scenarios; }
90.         }
91. 
92.         /// <summary>
93.         /// Display a message to the user.
94.         /// This method may be called from any thread.
95.         /// </summary>
96.         /// <param name="strMessage"></param>
97.         /// <param name="type"></param>
98.         public void NotifyUser(string strMessage, NotifyType type)
99.         {
100.             // If called from the UI thread, then update immediately.
101.             // Otherwise, schedule a task on the UI thread to perform the update.
102.             if (Dispatcher.HasThreadAccess)
103.             {
104.                 UpdateStatus(strMessage, type);
105.             }
106.             else
107.             {
108.                 var task = Dispatcher.RunAsync(CoreDispatcherPriority.Normal, () => UpdateStatus(strMessage, type));
109.             }
110.         }
111. 
112.         private void UpdateStatus(string strMessage, NotifyType type)
113.         {
114.             switch (type)
115.             {
116.                 case NotifyType.StatusMessage:
117.                     StatusBorder.Background = new SolidColorBrush(Windows.UI.Colors.Green);
118.                     break;
119.                 case NotifyType.ErrorMessage:
120.                     StatusBorder.Background = new SolidColorBrush(Windows.UI.Colors.Red);
121.                     break;
122.             }
123. 
124.             StatusBlock.Text = strMessage;
125. 
126.             // Collapse the StatusBlock if it has no text to conserve real estate.
127.             StatusBorder.Visibility = (StatusBlock.Text != String.Empty) ? Visibility.Visible : Visibility.Collapsed;
128.             if (StatusBlock.Text != String.Empty)
129.             {
130.                 StatusBorder.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;
131.                 StatusPanel.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;
132.             }
133.             else
134.             {
135.                 StatusBorder.Visibility = Visibility.Collapsed;
136.                 StatusPanel.Visibility = Visibility.Collapsed;
137.             }
138. 
139. 			// Raise an event if necessary to enable a screen reader to announce the status update.
140. 			var peer = FrameworkElementAutomationPeer.FromElement(StatusBlock);
141. 			if (peer != null)
142. 			{
143. 				peer.RaiseAutomationEvent(AutomationEvents.LiveRegionChanged);
144. 			}
145. 		}
146. 
147.         async void Footer_Click(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)
148.         {
149.             await Windows.System.Launcher.LaunchUriAsync(new Uri(((HyperlinkButton)sender).Tag.ToString()));
150.         }
151. 
152.         private void Button_Click(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)
153.         {
154.             Splitter.IsPaneOpen = !Splitter.IsPaneOpen;
155.         }
156.     }
157.     public enum NotifyType
158.     {
159.         StatusMessage,
160.         ErrorMessage
161.     };
162. }

<a id="windowing"></a>
## Windowing: Window.Current / ApplicationView / CoreWindow → AppWindow

`Window.Current`, `ApplicationView`, and `CoreWindow` are gone. Track the main window yourself and use `Microsoft.UI.Windowing.AppWindow`. See the official [windowing guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/windows/apps/windows-app-sdk/migrate-to-windows-app-sdk/guides/windowing).

UWP (replace this):

```csharp
var win = Window.Current;
ApplicationView.GetForCurrentView().TryResizeView(new Size(800, 600));
```

WinUI 3 — expose `MainWindow` from `App`:

```csharp
public partial class App : Application
{
    // With <Nullable>enable</Nullable>, declare as nullable since the assignment only happens during OnLaunched.
    public static Window? MainWindow { get; private set; }

    protected override void OnLaunched(LaunchActivatedEventArgs args)
    {
        var window = new MainWindow();
        MainWindow = window;
        window.Activate();
    }
}
```

Consumers running after `OnLaunched` can safely null-forgive:

```csharp
var hwnd = WinRT.Interop.WindowNative.GetWindowHandle(App.MainWindow!);
```

Resize via `AppWindow`:

```csharp
var hwnd = WinRT.Interop.WindowNative.GetWindowHandle(App.MainWindow);
var windowId = Microsoft.UI.Win32Interop.GetWindowIdFromWindow(hwnd);
var appWindow = AppWindow.GetFromWindowId(windowId);
appWindow.Resize(new SizeInt32(800, 600));
```

### Initialization order — keep MainWindow's constructor inert

`App.MainWindow` (or any equivalent static window reference) is `null` for the entire duration of `new MainWindow()` — the right-hand side runs to completion BEFORE the assignment happens. Anything inside that constructor that reads the static — directly or transitively — sees `null` and throws `E_POINTER` (0x80004003) at startup. The build is clean; the analyzer is silent; only a runtime launch reveals it.

APIs that commonly trip this race when called from a `Page` reached during `MainWindow` construction:

- `WinRT.Interop.WindowNative.GetWindowHandle(App.MainWindow)`
- `XamlRoot` / `Content.XamlRoot` access through a static-window helper
- `PrintManagerInterop.GetForWindow(hwnd)` / `ShowPrintUIForWindowAsync(hwnd)`
- `FileOpenPicker` / `FileSavePicker` / `FolderPicker` (require an HWND)
- `ContentDialog.ShowAsync()` when the dialog's `XamlRoot` is sourced from a static
- `AppWindow.GetFromWindowId(...)` chained through `App.MainWindow`
- Any custom title bar / `SetTitleBar` helper that resolves the HWND statically

Safe pattern — assign before activate, defer first navigation:

```csharp
// App.xaml.cs
protected override void OnLaunched(LaunchActivatedEventArgs args)
{
    var window = new MainWindow();
    MainWindow = window;
    window.Activate();
}
```

The `MainWindow` constructor must do only `InitializeComponent` and inert setup — no navigation to a `Page` that reads `App.MainWindow`. Defer the first navigation until after `Activate` completes, either from a `Loaded` handler hooked in `InitializeComponent` or by exposing a `NavigateToInitialPage()` method and calling it from `App.OnLaunched` after the static is assigned.

```csharp
// MainWindow.xaml.cs
public MainWindow()
{
    InitializeComponent();
    this.Activated += (_, _) =>
    {
        if (!_navigated)
        {
            _navigated = true;
            RootFrame.Navigate(typeof(MainPage));
        }
    };
}
private bool _navigated;
```

Validator catches this race with a 10s smoke launch after the build healthcheck passes — see `Validate-UwpMigration.ps1` Section 7.
<shellId: 4 completed with exit code 0>

<a id="navigationview-frame-wiring"></a>
### NavigationView + Frame wiring (SDK-sample scenario list)

The UWP SDK-sample idiom `MainPage` + `ListView`/scenario list + `Frame` maps to
`NavigationView` + `Frame` (SKILL.md shell-mapping table). Two **silent** failures are
common — they build and launch with no error, so nothing flags them:

1. **Blank Frame on every page** — a `SelectionChanged` type mismatch (see the wrong-cast
   warning below).
2. **Dead scenarios / first-page-sticks** — the *first* scenario renders but selecting any
   *other* nav item highlights it yet never switches the content `Frame`. This is the more
   dangerous one: it passes an alive-only smoke launch and a "does the first scenario
   render?" check, so it ships broken.

**Do NOT drive navigation from a constructor `SelectedItem` assignment.** Setting
`NavView.SelectedItem` in the constructor (before the item containers are realized) does
**not** reliably raise `SelectionChanged`, and for `MenuItemsSource` data items relying on
`SelectionChanged` alone to drive the `Frame` is unreliable — that is the exact cause of
the dead-scenario bug. Instead: **navigate the first item explicitly on `Loaded`, and
handle `ItemInvoked`** (which fires reliably on every click, for data-bound items too).

**When items come from `MenuItemsSource` (data-bound, e.g. `x:Bind Scenarios`):**
the invoked/selected item is the **bound data item**, NOT a `NavigationViewItem`. Navigate
off the data item's page type:

```csharp
public MainPage()
{
    InitializeComponent();
    Loaded += (_, _) =>                              // explicit initial nav — do NOT rely on SelectedItem
    {
        if (Scenarios.Count > 0)
        {
            NavView.SelectedItem = Scenarios[0];     // highlight only; navigation is explicit below
            ScenarioFrame.Navigate(Scenarios[0].ClassType);
        }
    };
}

private void NavView_ItemInvoked(NavigationView sender, NavigationViewItemInvokedEventArgs args)
{
    if (args.InvokedItem is string || args.InvokedItemContainer?.DataContext is not Scenario)
    {
        // InvokedItem is the bound data item's text; resolve the Scenario from the container.
    }
    if (args.InvokedItemContainer?.DataContext is Scenario s)   // data item, NOT NavigationViewItem
        ScenarioFrame.Navigate(s.ClassType);
}
```

Wire `ItemInvoked="NavView_ItemInvoked"` on the `NavigationView` in XAML. `ItemInvoked`
fires on every tap even when re-selecting, which is what keeps the switch reliable.

**When items are literal `NavigationViewItem`s in `MenuItems`:** the invoked item container
*is* a `NavigationViewItem`; read its `Tag`:

```csharp
private void NavView_ItemInvoked(NavigationView sender, NavigationViewItemInvokedEventArgs args)
{
    if (args.InvokedItemContainer is NavigationViewItem item && item.Tag is Type pageType)
        ScenarioFrame.Navigate(pageType);
}
```

Do not mix the two: casting a `MenuItemsSource` selection to `NavigationViewItem` (or
reading `NavView.MenuItems[0]` under `MenuItemsSource`) silently no-ops and yields the
blank-frame bug.

**Falsifiable check (must verify switching, not just first render):** after the shell loads,
the content `Frame` must contain the **first** scenario's controls; then, selecting the
**second** nav item must make the `Frame` show the **second** scenario's controls (its
distinct control names must appear in the UIA tree). If the first scenario renders but
switching to a second nav item leaves scenario-1 controls in the tree, the navigation is
broken — do not ship it.

<a id="csproj"></a>
## Project File Updates

- Target a current WinAppSDK-supported TFM. The exact TFM is the source of truth in the project's `.csproj`; do not hard-code it across instruction files. Typical values at time of writing:
  - `net8.0-windows10.0.19041.0` (LTS)
  - `net9.0-windows10.0.19041.0`
  - `net10.0-windows10.0.26100.0` (current `dotnet new winui` default in this repo)
- Add `<UseWinUI>true</UseWinUI>`.
- Add `<EnableMsixTooling>true</EnableMsixTooling>` for packaged builds.
- Reference `Microsoft.WindowsAppSDK` and `Microsoft.Windows.SDK.BuildTools`.
- Reference `Microsoft.Windows.SDK.BuildTools.WinApp` to wire `dotnet run` into `winapp run`.
- Keep `Package.appxmanifest` for packaged scenarios; set `<WindowsPackageType>None</WindowsPackageType>` for unpackaged.

### PackageReference reconciliation cheat-sheet

`Initialize-UwpMigration.ps1` preserves the UWP `.csproj` at `<Target>/.uwp-source/` and leaves the WinUI 3 scaffold's `.csproj` intact. Open both side-by-side and merge:

- **Drop** (UWP-only — never carry over):
  - `Microsoft.NETCore.UniversalWindowsPlatform`
  - Any `<PackageReference>` whose version string contains `uap` or whose name starts with `Windows.SDK.Contracts`.
- **Keep** (already in the scaffold — do not duplicate or downgrade):
  - `Microsoft.WindowsAppSDK`
  - `Microsoft.Windows.SDK.BuildTools`
- **Add** (project-specific — copy across, then update to a WinAppSDK-compatible version):
  - Third-party libraries the UWP project pulled in (`CommunityToolkit.*`, `Microsoft.Extensions.*`, `Newtonsoft.Json`, etc.). Pick the latest stable release; UWP-pinned versions are usually too old.

Do **not** copy the UWP `.csproj` over the scaffold's. The two formats are incompatible — the UWP csproj carries `<TargetPlatformIdentifier>UAP</TargetPlatformIdentifier>`, `<OutputType>AppContainerExe</OutputType>`, explicit `<Compile Include="...">` items, and `Microsoft.Common.props` imports, none of which build under WinAppSDK.

### Package.appxmanifest — reconcile image references with the assets you actually have

The WinUI 3 scaffold ships with a default `Package.appxmanifest` that references assets the template provides under `Assets/` (`SplashScreen.scale-200.png`, `Square150x150Logo.scale-200.png`, `Square44x44Logo.scale-200.png`, `StoreLogo.png`, `Wide310x150Logo.scale-200.png`, `LockScreenLogo.scale-200.png`). The UWP SDK sample's manifest usually points at sample-branded assets with a `-sdk` suffix (`Splash-sdk.png`, `squareTile-sdk.png`, `SmallTile-sdk.png`, `StoreLogo-sdk.png`).

If you copy the UWP manifest's `Logo` / `Square150x150Logo` / `Square44x44Logo` / `<uap:SplashScreen Image="...">` / `<uap:DefaultTile Wide310x150Logo="...">` references over the scaffold's without also bringing the matching files, **`winapp run` will register the package but the AppX layout will fail to launch** with errors like:

```
AppxManifest.xml(...): error 0x80070002: ... splash screen image [Splash-sdk.png] cannot be located.
```

(Failure code on the AppX side is typically `0x80073CF6` — "package could not be registered".)

You have two options. Pick one **before** the first `winapp run`:

- **Preferred — keep the scaffold's manifest image references** (`Assets\SplashScreen.scale-200.png`, etc.). When you merge any other content from the UWP manifest into the scaffold's (capabilities, file-type associations, `<uap:Extension>`, `<Application Id>`, etc.), do **not** overwrite the `Logo`, `<uap:SplashScreen Image>`, `Square150x150Logo`, `Square44x44Logo`, or `Wide310x150Logo` attribute values. The scaffold's defaults already match the files in its `Assets/` folder.

- **Alternative — keep the UWP sample's branded assets.** Copy every file the UWP manifest references from `.uwp-source/Assets/` (or `.uwp-source/<SampleName>/Assets/`) into the new project's `Assets/` folder, preserving the exact filename (including the `-sdk` suffix and any scale qualifiers). Verify by re-reading each `Image=`/`Logo>` value in the manifest and confirming `Test-Path "Assets\<that filename>"` for every one of them. Don't rename files to look prettier — the manifest reference is the source of truth.

Either way, before the first `winapp run`, sanity-check every asset reference in the manifest resolves:

```powershell
[xml]$m = Get-Content .\Package.appxmanifest
@($m.Package.Properties.Logo,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.Square150x150Logo,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.Square44x44Logo,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.uap_SplashScreen.Image,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.uap_DefaultTile.Wide310x150Logo
) | Where-Object { $_ } | ForEach-Object {
    if (-not (Test-Path $_)) { Write-Host "[MISSING] $_" -ForegroundColor Red }
}
```

### Manifest migration checklist (Windows.Desktop + runFullTrust)

The default UWP `Package.appxmanifest` declares itself as a Universal app, but a packaged WinUI 3 desktop app is a Win32 process with package identity — it needs a different shape. `winapp run` will register the AppX but **fail to deploy** with `0x80073CF6` or "requires runFullTrust capability" if any of the following are missing.

When merging the UWP manifest into the scaffold's, make sure all of these are true:

1. **TargetDeviceFamily is `Windows.Desktop`** — not `Windows.Universal`:
   ```xml
   <Dependencies>
     <TargetDeviceFamily Name="Windows.Desktop" MinVersion="10.0.17763.0" MaxVersionTested="10.0.22621.0" />
   </Dependencies>
   ```
   Windows.Universal is UWP-only and rejected for a Win32 entrypoint.

2. **rescap namespace declared on `<Package>` and added to `IgnorableNamespaces`**:
   ```xml
   <Package
     xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/appx/manifest/foundation/windows10"
     xmlns:uap="http://schemas.microsoft.com/appx/manifest/uap/windows10"
     xmlns:rescap="http://schemas.microsoft.com/appx/manifest/foundation/windows10/restrictedcapabilities"
     IgnorableNamespaces="uap rescap">
   ```
   Without the namespace declaration, item 3 below is silently stripped by AppX validation and you get the "requires runFullTrust" error at deploy time.

3. **`runFullTrust` capability declared**:
   ```xml
   <Capabilities>
     <rescap:Capability Name="runFullTrust" />
   </Capabilities>
   ```
   Packaged WinUI 3 desktop apps run outside the UWP AppContainer sandbox and must declare this. Keep any UWP `<Capability>` entries you actually use (e.g. `<DeviceCapability Name="webcam" />`) but add the `runFullTrust` line above no matter what.

4. **`<Application EntryPoint="$targetentrypoint$">`** — the WinUI 3 scaffold uses an MSBuild placeholder that the build resolves to the real entry point. Don't replace it with a literal `<UwpAppName>.App` (that's a UWP entry-point pattern).

5. **Carry over every UWP `<Extension>` the app's behavior depends on.** The scaffold manifest has none; the UWP manifest's `<Extensions>` are activation/registration prerequisites — omitting them makes the feature fail **at runtime** (often as a silently dead control), not at build time. Copy each `<Extension>` block from the UWP source manifest (under `.uwp-source/` or the copied UWP `Package.appxmanifest`) into the WinUI 3 root manifest's `<Application>`. Common ones:

   | Extension `Category` | Feature it powers |
   |----------------------|-------------------|
   | `windows.backgroundTasks` | out-of-process `BackgroundTaskBuilder` tasks (one `<Extension EntryPoint="...">` per `TaskEntryPoint`) |
   | `windows.protocol` | custom URI-scheme activation |
   | `windows.fileTypeAssociation` | open-with / file activation |
   | `windows.appService` | background app services |
   | `windows.shareTarget` | receiving shared content |

   `Validate-UwpMigration.ps1` FAILs if retained `BackgroundTaskBuilder` code has no `windows.backgroundTasks` extension; the other categories have no automated check, so verify them by diffing the UWP manifest's `<Extensions>` against the migrated one.

### WUI analyzer warnings (UWP API residue)

The benchmark's `winapp build` injects the `Microsoft.WindowsAppSDK.Analyzers` package, which flags UWP-only APIs that compile cleanly under WinUI 3 but throw `COMException` at runtime — typically inside `Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Application.Start(...)` before any window can render. The runner sees this as `builds=true, runs=false`, and `Validate-UwpMigration.ps1` will FAIL the build healthcheck for each unique warning.

| Rule | Symptom | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `WUI0002` | `Window.Current does not exist in WinUI 3 desktop apps` | Store the `Window` reference in `App.xaml.cs` (`App.Window`) and pass it where needed; see "Windowing" above. |
| `WUI0003` | `CoreDispatcher is UWP-only` | Use `DispatcherQueue.GetForCurrentThread()` and `TryEnqueue(...)`; see "Threading" above. |
| `WUI0004` | `SystemNavigationManager.GetForCurrentView() is UWP-only` | Drop the system back button hookup, or use HWND-based COM interop; see "GetForCurrentView Replacements" above. |

Treat every `warning WUI000\d` line in the build output as a defect — the analyzer does not produce false positives. Search for the API name in this document for the recommended replacement.

### Bootstrap-neutralized helper classes (filter-prone)

Some SDK Samples ship a shared `Common\NavigationHelper.cs` containing a `RootFrameNavigationHelper` class that wires ALT+Left / BrowserBack (virtual key 166) / mouse XButton1+XButton2 to frame back/forward navigation. Its WinUI 3 equivalent requires `Microsoft.UI.Input.InputKeyboardSource.GetKeyState` or P/Invoke `GetAsyncKeyState` plus virtual-key code constants — exactly the shape the model provider's content-safety filter classifies as "keylogger / surveillance" code. Migrating it routinely produces `CAPIError: 400 Output blocked by content filtering policy`.

`Initialize-UwpMigration.ps1` handles this automatically: before the agent ever sees the file, the bootstrap rewrites just the `RootFrameNavigationHelper` class body to a no-op stub (preserving the public ctor with `params object[] args` so callers compile no matter what signature they invoke). The audit log records `NEUTRALIZED  class RootFrameNavigationHelper`. The mapping row's Notes column is prefixed with `NEUTRALIZED:`.

**You don't need to migrate the neutralized class.** Just leave both it and the call site (typically `new RootFrameNavigationHelper(rootFrame)` in `App.xaml.cs`'s `OnLaunched`) untouched — back-nav is not a demonstrated feature of any UWP SDK sample. The remainder of `NavigationHelper.cs` (the `NavigationHelper` class used for per-page state save/restore, the LoadStateEventArgs / SaveStateEventArgs types) is migrated normally; it does not trigger the filter.

If you really want ALT+Left back-nav, add a single declarative XAML element to the AppBarButton that triggers GoBack:

```xml
<AppBarButton Icon="Back" Click="OnBackClick">
  <AppBarButton.KeyboardAccelerators>
    <KeyboardAccelerator Key="Left" Modifiers="Menu"/>
  </AppBarButton.KeyboardAccelerators>
</AppBarButton>
```

`KeyboardAccelerator` is declarative and does not trip the filter.
<shellId: 3 completed with exit code 0>
--- RESULT ---
<a id="threading"></a>
## Threading: CoreDispatcher → DispatcherQueue

UWP (replace this):

```csharp
await Dispatcher.RunAsync(CoreDispatcherPriority.Normal, () => StatusText.Text = "Done");
```

WinUI 3:

```csharp
DispatcherQueue.TryEnqueue(() => StatusText.Text = "Done");
DispatcherQueue.TryEnqueue(DispatcherQueuePriority.High, () => ProgressBar.Value = 100);
```

Cache the queue off the UI thread via `DispatcherQueue.GetForCurrentThread()`. UWP's ASTA reentrancy protection is gone — watch for reentrancy in async code that pumps messages. See the official [threading guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/windows/apps/windows-app-sdk/migrate-to-windows-app-sdk/guides/threading).

12. using Windows.Devices.Haptics;
13. using Windows.Devices.Input;
14. using Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Controls;
15. using Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Input;
16. 
17. // This sample shows how to play inking feedback tactile signals, how to
18. // adjust the intensity of the tactile signal, and how to fall back to a default
19. // waveform if the desired waveform is not supported by the current PenDevice
20. namespace SDKTemplate
21. {
22.     public sealed partial class Scenario3_InkingFeedback : Page
23.     {
24.         SimpleHapticsController hapticsController;
25.         PenDevice penDevice;
26.         SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback currentFeedback;
27. 
28.         public Scenario3_InkingFeedback()
29.         {
30.             InitializeComponent();
31.         }
32. 
33.         // The PointerEnter event will get fired as soon as Pointer input is received.
34.         // This event handler implementation will query the device providing input to see if it's a pen and
35.         // then check to see the pen supports tactile feedback and, if so, configure the PenDevice
36.         // to send the appropriate tactile signal based on what is selected in the dropdown.
37.         private void HapticCanvas_Entered(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
38.         {
39.             // If the current Pointer device is not a pen, exit
40.             if (e.Pointer.PointerDeviceType != PointerDeviceType.Pen)
41.             {
42.                 return;
43.             }
44. 
45.             // Attempt to retrieve the PenDevice from the current PointerId
46.             penDevice = PenDevice.GetFromPointerId(e.Pointer.PointerId);
47. 
48.             // If a PenDevice cannot be retrieved based on the PointerId, it does not support
49.             // advanced pen features, such as tactile feedback
50.             if (penDevice == null)
51.             {
52.                 statusText.Text = "Advanced pen features not supported";
53.                 return;
54.             }
55. 
56.             // Check to see if the current PenDevice supports tactile feedback by seeing if it
57.             // has a SimpleHapticsController
58.             hapticsController = penDevice.SimpleHapticsController;
59.             if (hapticsController == null)
60.             {
61.                 statusText.Text = "This pen does not provide tactile feedback";
62.                 return;
63.             }
64. 
65.             // Get feedback based on the user's selected waveform
66.             currentFeedback = GetSelectedFeedbackOrFallback(out string message);
67. 
68.             // Send the current feedback to the PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController.
69.             // Once sent, inking tactile feedback will be triggered as soon as the pen tip touches
70.             // the screen and will be stopped once the pen tip is lifted from the screen.
71.             // Also, check to see if the current PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController supports
72.             // setting the intensity value of the tactile feedback.  If so, set it based
73.             // on the slider.  If not, send the waveform without custom intensity.
74.             if (hapticsController.IsIntensitySupported)
75.             {
76.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback, intensitySlider.Value / 100);
77.                 message += "\nIntensity set to " + intensitySlider.Value + "%";
78.             }
79.             else
80.             {
81.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback);
82.                 message += "\nSetting intensity is not supported by this pen";
83.             }
84.             statusText.Text = message;
85.         }
86. 
87.         // Stop sending the tactile feedback and clear the current penDevice and hapticsController on PointerExit.
88.         // Stopping the feedback is important as it clears the tactile signal from the PenDevice's
89.         // SimpleHapticsController, ensuring that it has a clean state once it next enters range.
90.         private void HapticCanvas_Exited(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
91.         {
92.             penDevice = null;
93.             statusText.Text = "";
94.             if (hapticsController != null)
95.             {
96.                 hapticsController.StopFeedback();
97.                 hapticsController = null;
98.                 currentFeedback = null;
99.             }
100.         }
101. 
102.         // Get feedback which matches the currently selected waveform in the dropdown.
103.         // To get the feedback, the SimpleHapticsController's SupportedFeedback list must be traversed
104.         // to see if a matching feedback is present.
105.         // This is important because InkContinuous is the only Inking waveform required to be
106.         // supported by every pen with haptics.
107.         // If no matching feedback is found in the SupportedFeedback list, this method returns
108.         // InkContinuous as a guaranteed fallback.
109.         private SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback GetSelectedFeedbackOrFallback(out string message)
110.         {
111.             // Look up the waveform the user selected.
112.             string name = (string)waveformComboBox.SelectionBoxItem;
113.             ushort waveform = MainPage.WaveformNamesMap[name];
114. 
115.             // See if the haptics controller supports the selected waveform.
116.             SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback feedback = MainPage.FindSupportedFeedback(hapticsController, waveform);
117.             if (feedback != null)
118.             {
119.                 message = "Waveform set to " + name;
120.                 return feedback;
121.             }
122. 
123.             // It does not. Use InkContinuous as a fallback.
124.             message = name + " is not supported by this pen, so falling back to InkContinuous";
125.             return MainPage.FindSupportedFeedback(hapticsController, KnownSimpleHapticsControllerWaveforms.InkContinuous);
126.         }
127.     }
128. }
129.

82.         private void SendFeedback_Clicked(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)
83.         {
84.             if (hapticsController == null)
85.             {
86.                 return;
87.             }
88. 
89.             // Get feedback based on the user's selected waveform
90.             currentFeedback = GetSelectedFeedbackOrFallback(out string message);
91. 
92.             // Send the current feedback to the PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController.
93.             // Once sent, unlike inking feedback, this feedback will be played immediately.
94.             // Also, check to see if the current PenDevice's SimpleHapticsController supports
95.             // setting the intensity value of the tactile feedback.  If so, set it based
96.             // on the slider.  If not, send the feedback without custom intensity.
97.             if (hapticsController.IsIntensitySupported)
98.             {
99.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback, intensitySlider.Value / 100);
100.                 message += "\nIntensity set to " + intensitySlider.Value + "%";
101.             }
102.             else
103.             {
104.                 hapticsController.SendHapticFeedback(currentFeedback);
105.                 message += "\nSetting intensity is not supported by this pen";
106.             }
107.             statusText.Text = message;
108.         }
109. 
110.         // Get feedback which matches the currently selected waveform in the dropdown.
111.         // To get the feedback, the SimpleHapticsController's SupportedFeedback list must be traversed
112.         // to see if a matching feedback is present.
113.         // This is important because Click is the only Interaction waveform required to be
114.         // supported by every pen with haptics.
115.         // If no matching feedback is found in the SupportedFeedback list, this method returns
116.         // Click as a guaranteed fallback.

18. // This sample shows how to query the pen currently providing input to the system to see

20. namespace SDKTemplate
21. {
22.     public sealed partial class Scenario2_SupportedTactileFeedback : Page
23.     {
24.         SimpleHapticsController hapticsController;
25.         PenDevice penDevice;
26. 
27.         public Scenario2_SupportedTactileFeedback()
28.         {
29.             InitializeComponent();
30.         }
31. 
32.         // The PointerEnter event will get fired as soon as Pointer input is received.
33.         // This event handler implementation will query the device providing input to see if it's a pen and
34.         // then check to see the pen supports tactile feedback and, if so, what features it supports
35.         private void Pointer_Entered(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
36.         {
37.             // If the current Pointer device is not a pen, exit
38.             if (e.Pointer.PointerDeviceType != PointerDeviceType.Pen)
39.             {
40.                 supportedFeatures.Text = "";
41.                 supportedFeedback.Text = "";
42.                 return;
43.             }
44. 
45.             // Attempt to retrieve the PenDevice from the current PointerId
46.             penDevice = PenDevice.GetFromPointerId(e.Pointer.PointerId);
47. 
48.             // If a PenDevice cannot be retrieved based on the PointerId, it does not support
49.             // advanced pen features, such as tactile feedback
50.             if (penDevice == null)
51.             {
52.                 statusText.Text = "Advanced pen features not supported";
53.                 supportedFeatures.Text = "";
54.                 supportedFeedback.Text = "";
55.                 return;
56.             }
57. 
58.             // Check to see if the current PenDevice supports tactile feedback by seeing if it
59.             // has a SimpleHapticsController
60.             hapticsController = penDevice.SimpleHapticsController;
61.             if (hapticsController == null)
62.             {
63.                 statusText.Text = "This pen does not provide tactile feedback";
64.                 return;
65.             }
66. 
67.             // Check which tactile feedback features are supported
68.             supportedFeatures.Text = "Supported Haptics Features:\n";
69.             if (hapticsController.IsIntensitySupported)
70.             {
71.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "Intensity\n";
72.             }
73.             if (hapticsController.IsPlayCountSupported)
74.             {
75.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "PlayCount\n";
76.             }
77.             if (hapticsController.IsPlayDurationSupported)
78.             {
79.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "PlayDuration\n";
80.             }
81.             if (hapticsController.IsReplayPauseIntervalSupported)
82.             {
83.                 supportedFeatures.Text += "ReplayPauseInterval\n";
84.             }
85. 
86.             // Check which feedback waveforms are supported
87.             supportedFeedback.Text = "Supported Feedback:\n";
88.             foreach (SimpleHapticsControllerFeedback feedback in hapticsController.SupportedFeedback)
89.             {
90.                 ushort waveform = feedback.Waveform;
91.                 foreach (KeyValuePair<string, ushort> entry in MainPage.WaveformNamesMap)
92.                 {
93.                     if (entry.Value == waveform)
94.                     {
95.                         supportedFeedback.Text += entry.Key + "\n";
96.                         break;
97.                     }
98.                 }
99.             }
100.             statusText.Text = "";
101.         }
102. 
103.         // Clear the current penDevice and hapticsController on PointerExit
104.         private void Pointer_Exited(object sender, PointerRoutedEventArgs e)
105.         {
106.             penDevice = null;
107.             hapticsController = null;
108.         }
109.     }
110. }
111.

131.         StatusPanel.Visibility = isVisible ? Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Visibility.Visible : Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Visibility.Collapsed;
132.
